Comment Period Duration Comment: Commenters argued that the 30-day post-promulgation comment window was inadequate given the breadth and complexity of the rule, which alters established practices related to assessing and collecting civil penalties under the three INA provisions. Commenters also cited Executive Order 12866, Regulatory Planning and Review, 58 FR 51735, 51740, directing agencies to “afford the public a meaningful opportunity to comment on any proposed regulation, which in most cases should include a comment period of not less than 60 days” and argued that the abbreviated timeframe prevented comprehensive analysis of the rule’s many substantive changes.
답변: 우선, 논평자들은 의견수렴 기간으로 인하여 다루지 못하였던 추가적인 쟁점이 무엇인지 구체적으로 제시하거나 시사하지 않았습니다. 오히려 접수된 의견들은 그 폭과 상세함의 수준에 비추어 볼 때 해당 기간이 충분하고도 남음이 있었음을 보여줍니다. City of Waukesha v. EPA, 320 F.3d 228, 246 (D.C. Cir. 2003) 참조("통지 및 의견수렴 요건 위반과 관련하여 [불이익을 입증함에 있어], 청원인은 적절한 통지가 제공되었더라면 개정 규칙의 근거를 무효화할 수 있었을 추가적이고 상이한 의견을 제출하였을 것임을 입증하도록 요구될 수 있다.").
Response: As an initial matter, commenters did not suggest or indicate what additional issues the comment period precluded them from addressing; to the contrary, the comments received reflect both a breadth and a level of detail which suggests that the period was more than sufficient. Cf. City of Waukesha v. EPA, 320 F.3d 228, 246 (D.C. Cir. 2003) (“In [showing prejudice] in the context of a violation of notice-and-comment requirements, petitioners may be required to demonstrate that, had proper notice been provided, they would have submitted additional, different comments that could have invalidated the rationale for the revised rule.”).
행정절차법(APA)은 공공 의견수렴 기간에 대해 특정한 최소 기간을 규정하고 있지 않습니다. 각 기관은 제안규칙에 대해 통상 30일의 기간을 제공하며, 복잡하거나 경제적으로 중대한 규칙제정의 경우에는 그보다 더 긴 기간을 제공하기도 하지만, 동 법률은 각 기관이 이해관계인에게 서면 자료, 의견 또는 주장을 제출함으로써 참여할 수 있는 기회를 일반적으로 제공하도록 요구할 뿐입니다.
The APA does not prescribe any specific minimum duration for public comment periods. While agencies often provide a 30-day period for proposed rules, and sometimes longer for complex or economically significant rulemakings, the statute simply requires that agencies generally afford interested parties an opportunity to participate through submission of written data, views, or arguments.
비록 그렇게 할 의무가 없음에도 불구하고, 각 부처는 2025년 6월 27일 임시최종규칙(IFR) 공표 이후 30일간의 의견제출 기간을 제공하였으며, 2025년 7월 28일까지 의견을 접수하였습니다. 해당 기간은 대중이 임시최종규칙을 검토하고 의견을 제출할 수 있는 실질적인 기회를 제공하였습니다. 실제로 연방법원은 30일을 합리적인 의견제출 기간으로 추정해 왔습니다. Nat'l Lifeline Ass'n v. Fed. Commc'ns Comm'n, 921 F.3d 1102, 1117 (D.C. Cir. 2019) 참조. 이 사안에서 각 부처가 접수한 상세한 공개의견서들은 30일의 기간이 대중이 실질적으로 검토하고 충분한 정보에 기초한 의견을 제출하기에 충분하였다는 증거가 됩니다. 예를 들어 Little Sisters of the Poor Saints Peter and Paul Home, 140 S. Ct. at 2385 참조 (“[통지 및 의견제출의] 목적은 요컨대 공정한 통지에 있다.”) (인용 생략).
Although not required to do so, the Departments nonetheless provided a 30-day comment period following publication of the IFR on June 27, 2025, accepting comments through July 28, 2025. That period afforded the public a meaningful opportunity to review the IFR and provide input. Indeed, federal courts have presumed 30 days to be a reasonable comment period length. See Nat'l Lifeline Ass’n v. Fed. Commc'ns Comm'n, 921 F.3d 1102, 1117 (D.C. Cir. 2019). Here, the detailed public comments the Departments received are evidence that the 30-day period was sufficient for the public to meaningfully review and provide informed comments. See, e.g., Little Sisters of the Poor Saints Peter and Paul Home, 140 S. Ct. at 2385 (“The object [of notice and comment], in short, is one of fair notice.”) (citation omitted).
각 부처는 기한 내에 제출된 모든 의견서를 접수하여 신중하게 검토하였습니다.
The Departments received and carefully considered all timely submissions.
Providing a 30-day window for comments on a rule of limited procedural scope is consistent with longstanding administrative practice and the guidance reflected in Executive Orders 12866 and 13563, which encourage agencies to provide meaningful participation. The Departments’ post-promulgation comment process thus satisfied the APA’s requirements and furthered its purposes of transparency and public involvement, even though prior notice and comment were not legally required under 5 U.S.C. 553(a)(1) and (b)(A).
2. E.O. 12866 Analysis Comment: One commenter asserted that the IFR would impose substantial new economic burdens on affected aliens and their households, with potentially significant aggregate impacts. They contended that the IFR’s increased and accelerated civil monetary penalties, particularly daily fines approaching $1,000 for failure to depart following a final order of removal, could result in liabilities large enough to cause long- term financial hardship, including wage garnishment, asset seizure, and bankruptcy. They further stated that these costs would likely fall disproportionately on low-income individuals and mixed-status families, potentially creating spillover effects for U.S. citizen spouses and children and exacerbating economic insecurity in immigrant communities. The commenter also argued that the IFR may generate billions of dollars in annual penalties, and that DHS’s retention of penalty revenues could create institutional incentives to maximize fine issuance. They maintained that the IFR was implemented without sufficient economic analysis or public input and recommend alternatives such as means-based penalties, hardship waivers, or installment plans, independent review mechanisms, and pilot implementation to evaluate real world economic effects before nationwide implementation.
Response: The Departments do not agree that this rule needs additional economic analysis or that it was adopted without considering financial impacts. As previously discussed, the rule does not create any new fines, change who can be fined, or increase the dollar amounts Congress has already set; it only changes the internal process DHS uses to issue and review those fines. Any financial hardship, including possible collection actions, comes from the underlying law and from an alien’s choice to remain in the United States after being ordered to leave, not from these procedural updates. DHS’s goal is to encourage people to follow removal and departure orders and to deter unlawful entry, not to generate revenue, and DHS will cancel most failure to depart fines when eligible aliens use the CBP Home app to leave the country. OMB has reviewed the rule as significant under Executive Order 12866. DHS has weighed the limited added economic effects of these process changes against their enforcement benefits, and the Departments provided a 30‑day comment period and considered economic and other feedback before finalizing the rule.
IV. Severability The changes made by the IFR, as amended by this final rule, impact provisions that are not necessarily interrelated and can function independent of one another. As such, the Departments believe that most of the provisions of this final rule can function sensibly and independently of other provisions. Therefore, in the event that any provisions in this rule are invalidated by a reviewing court, the Departments intend the remaining provisions to remain in effect to the fullest extent possible.
V. Statutory and Regulatory Requirements A. Administrative Procedure Act The Departments have determined that this rule can take immediate effect notwithstanding the 30-day delayed effective date requirement in 5 U.S.C. 553(d). Generally, there is good cause for this rule to take immediate effect. See 5 U.S.C. 553(d)(3). This rule generally adopts the provisions of the IFR, with one change to help mitigate the potential effect of mail delays that could impact the amount of time an alien has to contest a civil penalty. See 5 U.S.C. 553(d)(1) (excepting from the delayed effective date requirements substantive rules that grant or recognize an exemption or relieve a restriction). The purpose of the 30-day waiting period is “to give affected parties time to adjust their behavior before the final rule takes effect.” Riverbend Farms, Inc. v. Madigan, 958 F.2d 1479, 1485 (9th Cir. 1992). Here, however, that purpose would not be served by delaying the effective date of this final rule or the rule’s amendment to account for mail delays because the public does not need advanced notice in order to conform their behavior to the rule.
This final rule largely adopts the provisions of the IFR, which took effect on June 27, 2025. Consequently, aliens and legal service providers who may represent such aliens have long been on notice of those procedures and processes this rule adopts. See United States v. Gavrilovic, 551 F.2d 1099, 1104 (8th Cir. 1977) (noting that the “legislative history of the APA” indicates that the waiting period “was not intended to unduly hamper agencies from making a rule effective immediately,” but intended “to ‘afford persons affected a reasonable time to prepare for the effective date of a rule . . . or to take other action which the issuance may prompt’” (citing S. Rep. No. 752, 79th Cong., 1st Sess. 15 (1946); H.R. Rep. No. 1980, 79th Cong., 2d Sess. 25 (1946))). Indeed, the Departments have consistently communicated an intent to finalize the IFR.59 The fact that the IFR’s effective date was recently stayed60 does not change the Departments’ determination— the brief period between the entry of the stay on October 5, 2026, and [INSERT DATE OF PUBLICATION IN THE FEDERAL REGISTER], does not alter the fact that the public operated under the rule’s provisions for more than a year and has long been on notice of the Departments’ intent to make them final.
Similarly, the change this final rule makes does not alter the calculus merely because it has not been part of the status quo under the IFR for the past year. The minor amendment is designed to benefit aliens by recognizing that an alien’s untimely appeal may nonetheless be accepted if the late appeal was caused by a mail delay. See 5 U.S.C. 553(d)(1). Even so, it relates solely to agency procedure and practice and thus is not 59 See 2026 Unified Agenda, RIN 1653-AA96, Imposition and Collection of Civil Penalties for Certain Immigration-Related Violations, https://www.reginfo.gov/public/do/eAgendaViewRule?pubId=202510&RIN=1653-AA96 [https://perma.cc/T87D-AN6A] (showing August 2026 as the expected timeline for publication of this final rule); Def.’s Opp’n to Pls.’ Mot. to Compel, Maria L. v. Mullin, No. 1:25-cv-13471 (DDC filed May 11, 2026), dkt. 69 (“Third, the Interim Final Rule (“IFR”) Plaintiffs challenge is currently the subject of ongoing final rulemaking. The agencies are reviewing comments, including the comment Plaintiff Immigration Legal Resource Center (“ILRC”) submitted, and will issue a final rule in due course.”). 60 See Maria L. v. Mullin, No. CV 25-13471-GAO, 2026 WL 2983111 (D. Mass. Sept. 30, 2026). subject to the 30-day effective date for “substantive rule[s]” under 5 U.S.C. 553(d). The procedural rule exception “covers agency actions that do not themselves alter the rights or interests of parties, although it may alter the manner in which the parties present themselves or their viewpoints to the agency.” JEM Broad. Co., Inc. v. FCC, 22 F.3d 320, 326 (D.C. Cir. 1994) (quoting Batterton v. Marshall, 648 F.2d 694, 707 (D.C. Cir. 1980)); see also Mendoza v. Perez, 54 F.3d 1002, 1023–24 (D.C. Cir. 2014). The amendment in this final rule does not change an alien’s right to appeal a penalty under the IFR’s 15-business day appeal deadline and therefore “impose[s] no new substantive obligations or burdens upon the parties’ rights and interests.” Am. Fed'n of Lab. & Cong. of Indus. Organizations v. Nat’l Lab. Rels. Bd., 57 F.4th 1023, 1043 (D.C. Cir. 2023) (quoting EPIC v. U.S. Dep’t of Homeland Sec., 653 F.3d 1, 6 (D.C. Cir. 2011).61 Rather, while the amendment directs aliens to provide evidence of a mail delay in connection with an untimely appeal, this simply establishes a clear process for the agency to evaluate such a claim. Therefore, this evidentiary requirement is merely a procedural step, not a substantive change to the right to appeal itself.
In finding good cause to bypass the 30-day delayed effective date requirement, the Departments have “balanc[ed] the necessity for immediate implementation against principles of fundamental fairness which require that all affected persons be afforded a reasonable [amount of] time to prepare for the effective date of [the rule].” Omnipoint Corp. v. FCC, 78 F.3d 620, 630 (D.C. Cir. 1996) (citation omitted). Here, the balance tips sharply in favor of immediate implementation. The United States Government and the American people have a strong interest in making the final rule effective immediately to ensure that DHS can continue implementing the IFR’s provisions. As discussed in Section I.B. of this preamble, the IFR has reduced agency resource burdens by 61 The Departments further maintain that the 15-day appeal deadline is likewise a procedural change, rather than a substantive one, for the reasons explained in the IFR. 90 FR 27454.
streamlining the civil penalty process and has allowed DHS to exercise its statutorily authorized civil penalty authority at scale in response to the large number of aliens who have unlawfully entered the United States or have failed to comply with removal and voluntary departure orders in recent years. In turn, this has allowed DHS to encourage greater compliance with such orders and provides DHS with another tool to disincentive future unlawful entries at the border.
B. Executive Order 12866 (Regulatory Planning and Review) Executive Order 12866, Regulatory Planning and Review, 58 FR 51735 (Sept. 30, 1993), and Executive Order 13563, Improving Regulation and Regulatory Review, 76 FR 3821 (Jan. 18, 2011), direct agencies to assess all costs and benefits of available regulatory alternatives and, if regulation is necessary, to select regulatory approaches that maximize net benefits. The Office of Management and Budget has determined this rule is significant under Executive Order 12866.
This final rule will allow DHS to more quickly impose a greater number of civil penalties on aliens who have unlawfully entered the United States and those who remain after a removal or voluntary departure order. DHS has not assessed the extent to which this final rule will result in an increase in civil penalties collected by the Government. DHS believes that this effort will reduce potential agency resource burdens by streamlining the process, discourage future unlawful entries, and encourage greater compliance with removal and departure orders.
C. Executive Order 14192 (Unleashing Prosperity Through Deregulation) This final rule is not an Executive Order 14192 regulatory action because it is being issued with respect to an immigration-related function of the United States. The rule’s primary direct purpose is to implement or interpret the immigration laws of the United States (as described in section 101(a)(17) of the INA, 8 U.S.C. 1101(a)(17)) or any other function performed by the United States Federal Government with respect to aliens. See OMB Memorandum M-25-20, Guidance Implementing Section 3 of Executive Order 14192, titled “Unleashing Prosperity Through Deregulation” at 5–6 (Mar. 26, 2025).
D. Regulatory Flexibility Act The Regulatory Flexibility Act of 1980 (“RFA”), 5 U.S.C. 601–612, as amended, requires federal agencies to consider the potential impact of regulations on small entities during rulemaking. A regulatory flexibility analysis is not required when a rule is exempt from notice-and-comment rulemaking. This final rule finalizes an IFR that was exempt from the notice-and-comment rulemaking. Therefore, a regulatory flexibility analysis is not required.
However, even assuming that this rule is not exempt from notice-and-comment procedures and therefore not exempt from the requirements of the RFA, the Departments have reviewed this rule in accordance with the RFA. The Secretary of Homeland Security and the Acting Director of EOIR certify that this rule will not have a significant economic impact on a substantial number of small entities. See 5 U.S.C. 605(b). First, aliens are not “small entit[ies]” as defined by the RFA. See 5 U.S.C. 601(6) (defining “small entity” as having the same meaning as “small business,” “small organization,” and “small government jurisdiction” as defined in 5 U.S.C. 601(3)–(5)). Second, to the extent that legal service organizations qualify as “small entities,” any economic effects of this rule on such organizations would not be significant under the RFA. The rule will not economically impact representatives of aliens in contesting these penalties. It does not limit the fees they may charge or the number of cases a representative may ethically accept under the rules of professional responsibility. For example, the IFR's changes to the procedures for contesting a civil penalty do not change the overall requirement that aliens must respond to a civil penalty notice to challenge the penalty; rather the IFR simply changed the procedures for how that appeal is filed, in what time frame, and which U.S. Government officials are responsible for adjudicating the appeal. The IFR’s changes to shorten the appeal period required legal service providers to adjust to these new timelines. The Departments expect, however, that legal service organizations have quickly acclimated to this change without substantial disruption. Indeed, the IFR was in effect for over a year, and the appeal rate increased under the IFR compared to the pre-IFR process for these civil penalties under 8 CFR part 280. Thus, any costs incurred in adapting to the IFR’s timelines were not significant under the RFA. And the one change in this final rule to the IFR’s provisions is not expected to increase such costs significantly (if at all) given that it discretely applies to demonstrated mail delays.
In any event, this rule does not limit or otherwise dictate the fees that legal service providers may charge or the number of civil penalty cases the organization may ethically and competently accept. See Mid-Tex Elec. Co-op., Inc. v. FERC, 773 F.2d 327, 342–43 (D.C. Cir. 1985) (concluding “an agency may properly certify that no regulatory flexibility analysis is necessary when it determines that the rule will not have a significant economic impact on a substantial number of small entities that are subject to the requirements of the rule” and stating that legislative history of the RFA “gives rise to an inference that Congress did not intend to require that every agency consider every indirect effect that any regulation might have on small businesses in any stratum of the national economy”).
Moreover, any effects this rule may have on legal service organizations, other representatives of aliens, or other entities that provide services to aliens subject to civil penalty procedures are indirect. The rule does not impose reporting, recordkeeping, staffing, fee, or other compliance requirements on those entities. Rather, it establishes procedures governing DHS’s issuance and review of civil penalties and the process by which an alien may seek administrative review of a penalty determination. Although a legal service organization or other representative may choose to adjust its internal practices in response to procedures or timelines applicable to its clients, any such effects result from the entity’s provision of services to affected aliens and not from a regulatory requirement imposed directly on that entity by this rule. The RFA does not require an agency to assess indirect effects on small entities that are not themselves subject to the rule’s requirements. Accordingly, the Departments do not treat such indirect effects as regulatory compliance costs imposed on small entities by this rule.62 E. Unfunded Mandates Reform Act of 1995 This final rule would not result in the expenditure by State, local, and Tribal governments, in the aggregate, or by the private sector, of $100 million or more in any one year, and it will not significantly or uniquely affect small governments. Therefore, no actions are deemed necessary under the provisions of the Unfunded Mandates Reform Act of 1995.
F. Congressional Review Act This final rule is not a “rule” as defined by the Congressional Review Act, Public Law 104–121. See 5 U.S.C. 804(3)(C) (defining the term “rule” to exclude “any rule of agency organization, procedure, or practice that does not substantially affect the rights or obligations of non-agency parties”). The Departments will nonetheless submit this final rule to both houses of Congress and the Comptroller General before the rule takes effect. G. Privacy Act In accordance with the Privacy Act of 1974, DHS/ICE-011 Criminal Arrest Records and Immigration Enforcement Records (CARIER) System of Records Notice provides privacy coverage supporting the final rule consistent with system purpose (“To 62 See U.S. Small Business Administration, Office of Advocacy, A Guide for Government Agencies: How to Comply with the Regulatory Flexibility Act 22–23 (Aug. 2017), https://advocacy.sba.gov/wp- content/uploads/2019/06/How-to-Comply-with-the-RFA.pdf (explaining that courts have held that the RFA requires analysis of impacts on small entities directly regulated by a rule, rather than indirect effects on entities that provide services to or otherwise interact with directly affected persons); Mid-Tex Elec. Coop., Inc., 773 F.2d at 342–43; see also Am. Trucking Ass’ns, Inc. v. EPA, 175 F.3d 1027, 1044 (D.C. Cir. 1999). track the process and results of administrative and criminal proceedings, including compliance with court orders and hearing dates, against individuals who are alleged to have violated the INA or other laws enforced by DHS”) and categories of records in the system. 89 FR 55638 (July 5, 2024).
Additionally, DHS/CBP-023 Border Patrol Enforcement Records, System of Records Notice provides coverage supporting the final rule consistent with system purpose (“Enforcement-related data including: Case number, record number, and other data describing an event involving alleged violations of criminal, immigration, or other laws (location, date, time, event category, types of criminal or immigration law violations alleged, types of property involved, use of violence, weapons, or assault against DHS personnel or third parties, attempted escape, and other related information); CBP encounter management information, including: Category (event categories describe broad categories of criminal law enforcement, such as smuggling and human trafficking), agent or officer, location of officer or officer’s vehicle, date/time initiated, date/time completed, assets used for encounter (bike, horse, vehicle, etc.), results of the encounter, and any agent or officer notes and comments.”), 81 FR 72601 (Oct. 20, 2016).
H. National Environmental Policy Act DHS and its components analyze final actions to determine whether the National Environmental Policy Act of 1969 (“NEPA”), 42 U.S.C. 4321 et seq., applies to them and, if so, what degree of analysis is required. DHS Directive 023-01 Rev. 0163 and Instruction Manual 023-01-001-01 Rev. 01 (“Instruction Manual”) establish the policies and procedures that DHS and its components use to comply with NEPA.
NEPA allows Federal agencies to establish categories of actions (“categorical exclusions”) that experience has shown do not, individually or cumulatively, have a 63 DHS, Implementation of the National Environmental Policy Act, Directive 023-01, Revision 01 (Oct. 31, 2014).
significant effect on the human environment and, therefore, do not require an environmental assessment (“EA”) or environmental impact statement (“EIS”). An agency is not required to prepare an EA or EIS for a proposed action “if the proposed agency action is excluded pursuant to one of the agency’s categorical exclusions.” 42 U.S.C. 4336(a)(2). The Instruction Manual, Appendix A, lists the DHS Categorical Exclusions. For an action to be categorically excluded under DHS’s Instruction Manual, the action must satisfy each of the following three conditions: (1) the entire action clearly fits within one or more of the categorical exclusions; (2) the action is not a piece of a larger action; and (3) no extraordinary circumstances exist that create the potential for a significant environmental effect.
This final rule is categorically excluded from DHS’s NEPA implementing procedures, because it satisfies all three relevant conditions. First, the Departments have determined that the IFR fits clearly within categorical exclusions A3(a) of DHS’s Instruction Manual, Appendix A, for the promulgation of rules of a “strictly administrative or procedural nature.” This final rule merely changes the procedures that DHS and DOJ apply when assessing civil monetary penalties authorized under certain sections of the INA. This change in procedures does not result in a change in their environmental effect. Second, this final rule is a standalone rule and is not part of any larger action. Third, the Departments are not aware of any extraordinary circumstances that would cause a significant environmental impact. Therefore, this final rule is categorically excluded, and no further NEPA analysis or documentation is required. I. Executive Order 13132 (Federalism) This final rule would not have substantial direct effects on the States, on the relationship between the national Government and the States, or on the distribution of power and responsibilities among the various levels of government. Therefore, in accordance with section 6 of Executive Order 13132, the Departments believe that this FR would not have sufficient federalism implications to warrant the preparation of a federalism summary impact statement.
J. Paperwork Reduction Act This final rule does not impose any new reporting or recordkeeping requirements or call for a collection of information under the Paperwork Reduction Act of 1995, 44 U.S.C. 3501, et seq.

Amendments to the Regulations DEPARTMENT OF HOMELAND SECURITY Accordingly, the interim final rule amending 8 CFR part 281, which was published at 90 FR 27439 on June 27, 2025, is adopted as final with the following change:
PART 281 – IMPOSITION AND COLLECTION OF PENALTIES UNDER SECTIONS 240B(d), 274D(a)(1), and 275(b) OF THE ACT
1. The authority citation for part 281 continues to read as follows:
Authority: 8 U.S.C. 1103, 1221, 1223, 1227, 1229, 1229c, 1253, 1322, 1323, 1325, 1324d, 1330; 5 U.S.C. 301; Public Law 107–296, 116 Stat. 2135 (6 U.S.C. 101 et seq.); 66 Stat. 173, 195, 197, 201, 203, 212, 219, 221–223, 226, 227, 230; Pub. 2. Amend 281.1, by redesignating paragraphs (e)(2) through (e)(4) as (e)(3) through (e)(5) and adding new paragraph (e)(2) to read as follows:
§ 281.1 Exclusive procedures for civil monetary penalties under sections 240B(d), 274D(a)(1), and 275(b) of the Act.
*****
(e) ***
(2) Appeals affected by mail delay. An alien who files an appeal after the 15- business days filing period prescribed in paragraph (e)(1) of this section and asserts that the untimely filing resulted from delayed or untimely mail delivery of the immigration officer’s decision must include with the appeal a written explanation and any available documentary evidence demonstrating the mail delay. An alien who files an appeal after the 15-business day filing period prescribed in paragraph (e)(1) of this section may also produce contemporaneous, time-stamped evidence of deposit of their mailed appeal directly from the U.S. Postal Service (“USPS”) or their chosen commercial carrier. If ICE determines that the appeal was filed after the deadline due to mail delay beyond the alien’s control, ICE will accept the appeal as timely filed and consider it on the merits. In making this determination, ICE will consider the mailing and actual receipt dates, the cause and length of the delay, and the alien’s good faith efforts in pursuing the appeal after receipt. ICE’s determination will be case by case considering all evidence presented with the appeal.
